Demo two is really how I was able to build what, to put it in a pithy way,
is a dating app for leads that we use internally at SpaceX AI,
where, you know, often, for those of you in the audience who know what I'm talking about,
marketing and sales handoff is, like, such a classic issue in companies,
where marketing is producing all these leads.
Salespeople are like, I don't really love these leads.
I don't want to have to work them.
So they're just sitting there getting stale.
You don't really have a feedback loop where marketing can refine their targeting
to give sales more of the kind of leads that they want.
And the whole process is just broken.
And so I sort of identified a better way to handle a marketing sales handoff,
which is what if every AE could log into an app
and they would just see the leads that they own?
They can swipe left to end those leads.
When they end those leads, they have to give a specific ended reason.
When they hit submit, it actually writes back to the CRM in all the correct ways.
When they swipe right, they can add that prospect.
They can add that lead directly into a follow-up sequence.
And so this is what I mean when earlier on I said,
this really transforms what we can do as someone on RevOps or MarOps,
where all of a sudden we're not just talking about SLAs
and making sure that the sales teams abide by the SLAs
and, hey, why aren't you working these leads?
And, okay, let me relay that anecdotal information

And really, some of the big lessons are if you were able to hire a full team, if someone came to you and said you have headcount to hire a full team of people reporting to you, who would be on it?
Really, what GrokBot does is it democratizes in a way that CEO life, anyone, can hire an unlimited set of specialists with the right context and access.
I think this is really powerful for our day-to-day in terms of just dreaming bigger about what we can each do for the teams that we're on.
The next lesson is really to trust your bots, give them agency, and at the same time, give them guardrails.
So there's something really powerful about knowing really confidently all the access that your bots have, all the things that they can do for you, because that helps you entrust them with bigger and bigger tasks.
But at the same time, knowing that things aren't going to go too far off the rails, because they'll always come back to you before they ship anything.
Messages, writing back to the CRM, pushing PRs.
And I think GrokBot really helps you achieve that balance between pushing AI tools to the limit and then running into issues, or feeling like, I don't really know what access it has, I don't know how helpful it can really be, so I haven't really started using it yet.

I think of it, I really think of it a lot as a mirror.
Oftentimes when I'm talking to bots, especially in one of those thought partnership sessions like you saw with the Q&A,
I think of the bot as, of course, in some ways, it's influencing how I think about what I'm trying to do.
So like it might bring up certain aspects of the build for that app that I didn't think of myself.

And so you can think of it as like, if nothing else, you're sort of like thinking out loud with these bots.

So that's what I think about like the relationship between me and the bot or anyone who uses GrokBot.
In terms of the stack that I think will go away, it's sort of hard to say.
I think there will probably still be many instances of like it's really good to just understand the right buttons to click and menus to navigate to use certain complex tools.
But I think something that's sort of happened, especially with coding, is like the barrier to entry is so much lower.
I think the engineering profession had this sort of reckoning over the past few years that more and more professions are going to start to have with the introductions of these kind of tools around like, okay, what do I do if my role is no longer just being the gatekeeper of this like really complicated looking code that no one else understands?
Like now all of a sudden anyone can produce that code.
And so what's the value add of an engineer?
There is a value add.
There definitely is.
But that value add is more about that higher level thinking, that intuitive feel for the systems.
And so I think it's helpful to really get to know the tools deeply because of, similar to before, like what it prompts you to think about.
Like no one really wants to be a master at navigating menus, but there's something about that tedious work that really makes you slow down and think about what you're trying to do in those complex tools, just like AI also prompts you to slow down and think.
So I think that is what's going to remain, I think, using complex tools to the extent that it gives you deep familiarity with your profession and expertise and taste, I think that will persist.
But I also think that in many ways there will be a lot less button clicking and like wiring things up properly and setting up the integrations.

What's really nice about these systems when you get bots talking to your other tools is that they can use those tools just like humans would.
Um, but they're often a lot faster at using them than humans are.
And so one common workflow that we use a ton internally at SpaceX AI is we have feedback channels and issues channels for our products.
Anytime someone posts an issue, we have triage box whose job it is to go in, take a look at the issue, see if it's reproducible, look at some logs in our internal logging systems, kind of come back with, well, is this something that needs a fix?
Uh, do I need to tag the right person who may, who might be able to help advise on this?
Uh, and those are really cool systems because it can really sort of, it can sort of shorten the time period between something being reported and then, you know, getting to a fix.
And very often saves a lot of engineering work where someone has to go manually look through a bunch of logs.
So we're trying to replicate some of those behaviors for this app, basically creating a Slack space, making it so that anytime we see an issue or any time one of you reports an issue, we can just automatically fire a bot.
To take a look at that, see if it's a real issue reproducible, look at logs and then kind of come back with a PR, uh, versus just coming back with an investigation or a dashboard that someone else has to go and write some code for.
So that, you know, earlier today, you heard us talking about the software factory.
Uh, and I think that's really what that is in practice.
It's sort of this automated software development loop, um, you know, and really for, for production apps, apps that are out in the field.
Uh, it's so important, right? Because, you know, no, no software is perfect.
Um, you know, but we don't, we don't intentionally put bugs in software, but bugs exist.
And so a large part of the work of an engineering team is kind of looking at issues that exist, uh, kind of making sure that we're seeing those issues come in, being able to triage them, fix them, and then continue to do that while evolving the product.
And so you'll see another set of issues. Well, hopefully not too many, but we're going to, we're trying to, we're working to minimize issues, but we are also doing a UI rewrite.
So I don't know.
We rewrote the UI.
We just, uh, revamped, uh, our, our authentication stuff.
So when you flip from a development to production kind of authentication, there's a bunch of OAuth and callback stuff you have to reconfigure.
And, uh, I don't know, it's an interesting lesson because, you know, a big part of not just building a business, but building like software is connecting things together and figuring out like production environments.
And like what production is, how that's different from a development environment. Um, and yeah, it's not always like fun and just like making stuff happen.
Sometimes it's like literally figuring out the glue, but agents are quite good for that because, you know, previously you would have had to diagnose these issues manually, which if anybody's tried to do this before, it takes a lot of time.
And so, um, having an agent understanding, you know, I think a larger point is, is all of the same skills that apply in like software engineering or running a business still apply.
So if you think about like what it means to be able to debug something like previously, that was like, okay, I'm going to understand all of the steps in a process.
And then I'm going to go by, go through those manually one by one until I kind of identify an error point.
Now we can, we can lean on agents to help us do that, but all the principles are kind of still the same, which is like between point A and point B, there are X number of things that can go wrong.
And how do I think about where we are, where we're going and then where all of those sort of points are right.
And I often describe this like, you know, fixing like a lamp.
Maybe if a lamp is not working, the first question you should ask is, is it, is the light bulb working or is the bulb burnt out and is it plugged in?
And if you can diagnose those two issues and they're, they're both working, then the issue is somewhere in between those two.
Right. And then your job as an engineer or someone trying to fix this lamp, someone who's trying to do it themselves is to figure out what point in that chain can go wrong.
And this is exactly the same with software.
So, you know, we have a lot of like API keys and things floating around, which is why we're kind of talking through this.
But these are good, good lessons. And it's the same thing in business. Right. So while you were talking, I know you've been working on getting the clerk app working in production.

So, so our backend is built with Go and it's actually a serverless backend built with Vercel functions.

And so one thing I was trying to think about last night was how do I make sure that the backend and the frontend, the client like stay in sync.
So, you know, when you're backend talk to your frontend, it would be talking over some protocol.
So for us, we just use a rest protocol, but so one thing I was trying to think about was type safety.
So type safety is this idea of, you know, on the backend, like let's say you have a bot type, right?
And the bot has certain fields, like, you know, it has a display, a display name.
It has our avatar.
It has some stats associated with it.
And let's say, you know, as Matt, Roshan and myself are contributing to the code base,
how do we ensure that if I make a backend change and Roshan makes a frontend change that we don't conflict?
You know, like maybe Roshan redesigns the card and I removed a field, right?
And now the field that the UI is reading from is no longer able to get that data.
So one of the things that I started to look at was like, how do we ensure that type safety over the network?
And I honestly didn't really have a, if I had spent more time, I might have designed this differently.
But for now, it's kind of a basic approach where we parse the responses we get from the backend through ZOD.
ZOD is this really great library, JavaScript library, and it essentially lets you parse the response and handle them and make sure that it's all validated and correct.

So funnel metrics are really great for that, particularly for zero to one products where you want to get a sense of how things are going.
And then obviously, you know, for a scale revenue product, which hopefully we can get to as well.

So verification swarms are essentially…
So the swarm skill in Pstack is a skill that teaches your bot how to spawn many cloud agents in Cursor.
And so these cloud agents will have their own computer, you know, they're kind of separate from the GrokBot computer.
And they will be able to run the game on their own machines and actually click around and test things and fuzz it.
So this is like a thing we love to say at SpaceX AI is like we're fuzzing.
And when we say fuzzing, we just mean like you're actually using the application like a real power user.
You're clicking every little button.
You're trying every little edge case.
You're really trying to stress test and break the game.
And so the idea here is that if we have a swarm of bots or agents that can actually do that for us, it takes us humans out of the loop there.
Because I don't have to just sit here and click and play the game and test it and find the bugs.
I can have my bots do that for me.

My name is Cal Day.
I am the SVP of product and engineering for one of our product development divisions at Nokia.
We are trying to leverage cursor as an agentic platform to introduce specialized agents that are persona specific for every aspect of work that we do that can partner with the human in seat that does that work today.
Those things become much more possible if we are able to iterate in much, much faster loops that directly impacts customer economics, lowers unit cost, improves customer satisfaction.
And then finally, there's an entire class of things that I think we're not going to be able to do faster that are to do with increasing resilience, reliability and security in the network.
Cursor and cursors agents that increasingly many of them are out of the box is a massive step up in accelerating analysis, getting insights that otherwise would not be possible.
So we're at the beginning of a very critical journey.
We've burnt the boats.
Our plans, our goals for this year, not next year, for this year, hinge on this working.
We have a product line, easily 50 plus million lines of code.
We are, I would say, in an intermediate stage of decomposing what is fundamentally a monolith oriented architecture.
It would be a very daunting task for us to attempt a manual inspection of that code base.
We have undertaken that with cursor.
Our early results of that analysis have been remarkable.
We have been able to do precisely what I just described, which would have required bespoke tooling, would have taken far longer, probably several months of effort across probably a dozen or more expert people.
We performed the initial analysis as one example within a matter of, I think, a couple of weeks by two people.
We've seen evidence of cursor being deployed very successfully in situations that involve a need to move very quickly where we have run into a customer impacting defect that might be having a subscriber impact.
And now we're able to do that against an extremely large corpus of service requests and radically accelerate something like a root cause analysis.
These examples have not been one or two.
There have been quite a few and growing in number.
That's where we're seeing, I'd say, the early success.
And where we would love to take it next is leveraging cursor as a platform for multi-agent deployment and orchestration, where really the role of the engineer, the architect becomes the supervision of agents that are calling out the tools that are performing work at scale, collaborating with one another.

So there are two main use cases.

The two main use cases are really keeping myself sane and reducing the context switching.
And so I like so many people have landed on this paradigm of a chief of staff agent.
And for me, I think the real unlock that takes it past like the chief of staff just being who I talked to to try getting anything done is the fact that I gave my chief of staff agent access to all my inboxes.
Like not just a couple, but truly everything.
How many inboxes do you have?
I have more than you might think.
It's like all my email addresses, my calendars, even like different chat accounts.
And so really across personal and professional use cases, I have all this information coming at me.
And of course, there's Slack.
And I think number one in today's day and age is distilling all of that, figuring out, okay, what are the tasks?
What are just the FYI is of the things that are tasks?
How do I get stuck into them?
And so what my chief of staff does, which I think is maybe a bit differentiated than the typical use case, is it can handle the whole end to end.
I call it a self-completing task list.
And so it's tapped into all my inboxes.
It receives what's coming into those inboxes on a regular basis.
It determines what subset of that information is tasks.
And then it identifies which of my other specialist bots are best positioned to take a first stab at those tasks.

So there are certain things that cost more money than others.

More of a costly option than if you go get everything that you want and just put it into Google Forms AI and it built it for you.
So using the VM can be a little bit more costly.

I was saying this earlier.

Yeah, well, we're in round two, but maybe I'll just start from the beginning.
So this is sort of like an auto-battler game is where we ended up.
Where you basically draft a team of bots.
And the bots are...
You sort of build this team of bots and they get lined up or matched up against another team of bots.
Every bot has a couple of stats.
So they have a sort of name, of course.
They have an ability that they get that's sort of randomly associated with the bot.
And they have an attack and an HP score and a CSS bug that we can also fix.
But attack and HP scores.
And the way these bots work is basically each turn you sort of set your lineup.
And the lineup is an ordered lineup where you put your bots one after the other after the other.
Three bots in a line.
And you're matched up with an opponent's bot lineup.
And sort of every turn the abilities and the effects was all one by one.
Whichever team has the most bot standing at the end wins the round.
If you win two out of three, you win the game.
So that's the core premise here.
The way that you actually decide how to set up your lineup is that you use gold.
So we actually probably should tweak the gold mechanic.
I saw some good feedback in coming in around how this works.

Let's jump in.
So let's start with the context.
The way we all started using AI was pretty simple.
Many of you may still be using AI today, just like this.
And I certainly was in this camp before I started on the SpaceX AI team.
The typical pattern is that you have one or two AI chatbots that you really use and leverage as your thought partner, meaning you chat with them, you ask questions, you spar on strategy, maybe you edit copy.
But they're really there as your thought partner and not a doing partner.
As AI has started advancing, there's been this rise of generalized and specialized agents that can start to do tasks with you.
We refer to these internally as co-pilots, because while they can get those tasks done with you, they still typically require you to babysit them, push them along, particularly for those complex tasks, rather than truly being able to delegate your work.
And that's the reason why we're so excited about GropBot.
With GropBot, marketing teams can now actually start to automate away true pieces of their work.
You can think of GropBot as always-on, asynchronous AI teammates that actually do and finish the work on your behalf.
And most importantly, they'll do it the way that you do it.
And today, I'm excited to demo how on the SpaceX AI marketing team, we not only use bots to automate jobs and tasks, but we actually use them to staff up functions and create teams of bots to actually do the work for you.
But first, let's do a quick intro of the product. For those of you who haven't tried it out yet, this is GropBot.
And now some of you may be wondering, how is GropBot different from all the other AI tools and chatbots that I might use today?
What's different about GropBot is that rather than working with one agent or one chat with multi-threaded conversations, you create individual bots for each job.
And what that means is that you think of one bot to be a singular teammate that you then delegate away a single task or a set of related tasks.
What this also means is that you can actually message your bots and talk to them just like you would Slack or message someone on Teams.
And to take this step further, bots can start to talk to each other and actually collaborate with each other within group chats as well.
And as you continue to work with your bots, they'll learn how you work, they'll learn your preferences, they'll learn your memory, and they'll get smarter and smarter and get closer to the way that you actually do the job yourself.
So, similar to someone on your team or someone that you collaborate with, the more feedback you give and the more you invest in your bots, the more they'll be able to do for you in the long term.
And what makes bots especially unique is that while it can hook up to all the MCPs and APIs that you're currently using today, it also has its own computer.
And that's a bit of a novel paradigm shift from how you might be using other AI tools today.
And what it simply means is that because it has access to its own browser and its own computer, so long as you give it access to tools that you use, it can actually use those tools just like you would.
So, what does this all mean for marketers?
If we talk about how it can use tools just like you, that means that your bot, you can teach it specific ways that you like to optimize your bidding strategy in ad platforms, or the nuances in how you put together a campaign brief, or maybe just how you like to manage your computer.
But ultimately, what's really unique and valuable about bot is that it becomes customized and flexible to the way that you work.
And with bot, you'll also find that you're no longer having to babysit and push along and really remind the bot to keep working.
And rather, Grokbot is extremely ambitious, and it's also very proactive.
And so now you'll be working with a team of bots that actually want and will finish the work for you.
And that's quite different from other tools you might be using.
Because bot is hosted in the cloud, that also means that they're running 24/7, no more keeping your laptop open or needing a Mac mini setup.
And for marketers, that means if you have reporting or metrics that you're looking at on a regular basis, you can trust that Grokbot will be able to pull those down for you.
And the best of all, as you might have noticed, it's really simple to use.
This UI is as familiar and intuitive as iMessage, which really lowers the barrier to entry for any marketer, no matter what their specialization is, to weave agents into their daily workflows.

So just to wrap that up, what have we learned by going through an entire campaign build from start to end?
The first point is what's really important when you're starting off with GrokBot is to ensure that you're scoping your bots properly.
And the way that I like to think about this is that when you identify a bot or a job that needs to be done, it's almost like crafting a job description.
Meaning you're identifying a new teammate that you need help from.
You understand the swim lanes and roles and responsibilities.
And you're ensuring that it's scoped tightly to ensure that they are specialized and they're able to get that specific piece of work done.
Because that's the way you squeeze the most efficiency out of your team of bots.
Second, you want to trust your bots.
As you might have seen, these teammates are ambitious.
They're proactive.
They're hungry.
They want to do your work.
And that starts with giving them the access they need.
Meaning one of the first steps you might take is hooking them up into your Slack instance, your email, and actually maybe asking it like, hey, go study all the context, all the messages, all of my organizational history, and tell me what you can do for me.
And take a job off my plate.
But I think if you do that, you give them the access, you throw ambitious tasks against them, you'll be surprised by what they're capable of.
And finally, I've said this over and over, but you want to invest in your bots.
They really get better and better over time.
You know, the point of collaboration between bot to bot is something that gets more organic, especially as you prompt it.
But they also improve steadily as you start to feed it more feedback, more context, more memory, similar to how you might work or manage your teammate.
Where the more feedback you give them, the more time you invest into them, the more they're compounded in value and be able to take more and more off your plate.
Lastly, here's a plug.
This QR code goes to our bot marketplace.
And the marketplace is actually where we take the very best bot templates, like the ones that I shared, and host them.
Meaning you might be able to create your own bots and prompt them and build up, you know, really rich context and memory.
But you can also just copy others.
And that's what I did, is when building out this demo, I crowdsourced across the entire team.
I said, hey, who has the best positioning bot, who has the best ads bot, et cetera.
And you can find all of those on our marketplace.
So I can encourage you to go here and be able to copy them down.
And with that, that's it for me.

I thought about this the other day.
Before I joined Cursor, I wrote this article on how I'm using Cursor.
And the main piece was how I'm using tab and the composer mode in the old Cursor.
And this was pre-agent.
And going from that to agent and then abstracting all the prompting myself to now using GrockBot.
So I started in GrockBot all the time.
And then I try to scope out the project and get all the context and align on what we're gonna do.
And then I'll just have it send a bunch of cloud agents to build the thing.
And eventually, I might want to look at the code, so I pull it locally.
And then I open Cursor and go through it all, see how it works.
And if I'm fine, I just go back to GrockBot and keep prompting from there.
So it's very much abstracted compared to what it used to be.
And relying heavily on reproducing things and computer use and just doing all the hard work and ironing out all the bugs before I'm taking a look at it.
So I don't really need to spend that much time to look at the code.
I only want to look at the code if it actually works the way I want.

I guess one thing that I would say to your question is I think there's just a lot of wisdom in the world.
Like we're really lucky to have a bunch of guests, as Matt called out, that kind of helped steer us throughout these three days.
But a lot of the bots we created to go learn things like we have game designer bots who learned how to do game design by just learning from the best games.
And one of the things that maybe I undervalued before this was you can give the bots lots of context, right?
You can sort of teach them all the things they need to know.
We had someone earlier on the stream talking about how whenever they're entering into a new discipline, they tell the bot to go take a bunch of courses on the Internet.
And I never really thought I never thought of that idea.
But you sort of think of these bots because they sort of learn and evolve over time.
You can kind of, you know, encourage them to learn the things you want them to learn.
Maybe another angle to what Matt said is create bots that help cover the things you don't know.
So if you don't really know how maybe hot food versus cold food works, you know, if we'd stuck with that idea, we could have probably tried to get a bunch of bots learning as much as they could about that and maybe giving us some advice.
And so I think my big thing would be don't be afraid to assemble a really varied team of bots and kind of think of them the way you'd think of like hiring a bunch, you know, a group of employees.
I think for me, actually, you know, we obviously this is a live stream, but actually the thing I think is even more valuable in the in the era of agents is actually the people that you work with.

I can't recall exactly what the post was, but it was something along the lines of, you know, every team needs basically two different types of people.

And then the other person you need is someone who is like relentless at making things happen and even better when you can find someone who can do both.
And I think that's a distinctly like human role that will continue to play even as models get more and more capable.
